# QRE-QA-A — quantitative-aptitude — SME review sheet (390 Q)

Levels {'L1': 52, 'L2': 90, 'L3': 224, 'L4': 24} · key positions {'C': 97, 'D': 97, 'B': 98, 'A': 98} · microtopics covered 26/26
Status `ai_drafted` / `draft`. ⚠ = verify_fact (statute / rate / threshold — check against current official text).

## QAA-001 · L1 · easy · Income, expenditure and savings · foundation

Meena earns ₹48,000 a month and spends 72% of it. Her monthly savings are:

- **A.** ₹18,240  _(error: subtraction slip: 100 − 72 taken as 38)_
- **B.** ₹34,560  _(error: expenditure reported instead of savings)_
- **C.** ₹13,440 ✅
- **D.** ₹9,676.80  _(error: 28% applied to expenditure instead of income)_

**Working**

1. Savings share = 100% − 72% = 28%
2. Savings = 28% of 48,000 = ₹13,440

**Formula:** Savings = Income − Expenditure  
**Trap:** The savings rate is the complement of the spending rate, applied to income.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-002 · L1 · easy · Income, expenditure and savings · foundation

A clerk saves ₹9,000 a month, which is 15% of his income. His monthly expenditure is:

- **A.** ₹69,000  _(error: savings added to income)_
- **B.** ₹1,350  _(error: 15% taken of savings)_
- **C.** ₹60,000  _(error: income reported instead of expenditure)_
- **D.** ₹51,000 ✅

**Working**

1. Income = 9,000 × 100/15 = ₹60,000
2. Expenditure = 60,000 − 9,000 = ₹51,000

**Formula:** Income = Savings ÷ savings rate  
**Trap:** Find income first; expenditure is income minus savings.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-003 · L2 · medium · Income, expenditure and savings · foundation

A person saves 25% of his income. His income rises by 20% and his expenditure by 10%. By what percentage do his savings increase?

- **A.** 30%  _(error: sum of the two rates)_
- **B.** 15%  _(error: average of the two rates)_
- **C.** 10%  _(error: difference of the two rates (20 − 10))_
- **D.** 50% ✅

**Working**

1. Take income 100, expenditure 75, savings 25
2. New income 120, new expenditure 82.5, new savings 37.5
3. Increase = 12.5/25 × 100 = 50%

**Formula:** Savings = Income − Expenditure; % change on old savings  
**Trap:** Savings react more than income because expenditure is a large fixed-share block.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-004 · L2 · medium · Income, expenditure and savings · foundation

The incomes of A and B are in the ratio 5 : 4 and their expenditures in the ratio 3 : 2. If each saves ₹8,000, A's income is:

- **A.** ₹40,000  _(error: savings multiplied by A's ratio term)_
- **B.** ₹12,000  _(error: A's expenditure reported)_
- **C.** ₹16,000  _(error: B's income reported)_
- **D.** ₹20,000 ✅

**Working**

1. Let incomes 5x, 4x and expenditures 3y, 2y
2. 5x − 3y = 8000 and 4x − 2y = 8000
3. Solving: x = 4,000, y = 4,000
4. A's income = 5x = ₹20,000

**Formula:** Income − Expenditure = Savings for each person  
**Trap:** Two different multipliers are needed for the two ratios.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-005 · L2 · medium · Income, expenditure and savings · foundation

Ravi spends 30% of his income on rent and 40% of the remainder on food. He saves the rest, which is ₹12,600. His income is:

- **A.** ₹42,000  _(error: 40% taken on income, not on the remainder)_
- **B.** ₹31,500  _(error: savings equated to 40%)_
- **C.** ₹30,000 ✅
- **D.** ₹18,000  _(error: food expense ignored)_

**Working**

1. After rent: 70% of income
2. Food = 40% of 70% = 28%; savings = 70% − 28% = 42%
3. Income = 12,600 ÷ 0.42 = ₹30,000

**Formula:** Chain the shares: 0.70 × 0.60 = 0.42  
**Trap:** 'Of the remainder' means the base shrinks after each spend.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-006 · L3 · hard · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

Arun's income rises by 25% and his expenditure by 15%; as a result his savings rise by 45%. What percentage of his original income did he save?

- **A.** 20%  _(error: the 20-point gap in growth rates read as the savings share)_
- **B.** 51.92%  _(error: savings growth applied to expenditure (125 − 1.15E = 1.45E))_
- **C.** 66 2/3%  _(error: expenditure share reported)_
- **D.** 33 1/3% ✅

**Working**

1. Income 100, expenditure E, savings 100 − E
2. 125 − 1.15E = 1.45(100 − E)
3. 0.30E = 20 ⇒ E = 66 2/3
4. Savings share = 33 1/3%

**Formula:** New income − new expenditure = new savings  
**Trap:** Set up the equation in savings, not in expenditure.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-007 · L3 · hard · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

A family saves 20% of its income. Income increases by 30% and savings increase by 10%. Expenditure increases by:

- **A.** 28%  _(error: expenditure increase measured on income instead of old expenditure)_
- **B.** 35% ✅
- **C.** 20%  _(error: difference of rates 30 − 10)_
- **D.** 40%  _(error: sum of rates)_

**Working**

1. Income 100, savings 20, expenditure 80
2. New income 130, new savings 22, new expenditure 108
3. Increase = 28/80 × 100 = 35%

**Formula:** % change = change ÷ old expenditure × 100  
**Trap:** Base for the expenditure change is 80, not 100.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-008 · L3 · hard · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

A's income is 25% more than B's and A's expenditure is 20% more than B's. A saves 40% more than B. What percentage of his income does A save?

- **A.** 25%  _(error: B's savings rate reported)_
- **B.** 28% ✅
- **C.** 72%  _(error: A's expenditure share reported)_
- **D.** 35%  _(error: A's savings measured on B's income)_

**Working**

1. B: income 100, expenditure E; A: income 125, expenditure 1.2E
2. 125 − 1.2E = 1.4(100 − E) ⇒ E = 75
3. A saves 125 − 90 = 35, i.e. 35/125 = 28%

**Formula:** Savings = Income − Expenditure for each person  
**Trap:** Measure A's savings rate on A's own income.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-009 · L2 · medium · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

From a monthly income of ₹75,000, Kavya spends 20% on housing, 25% of the remainder on education and 10% of what is then left on travel. She saves the balance. Her savings are:

- **A.** ₹37,500  _(error: travel taken as 10% of income)_
- **B.** ₹45,000  _(error: travel step missed)_
- **C.** ₹40,500 ✅
- **D.** ₹33,750  _(error: all three rates deducted from income (55%))_

**Working**

1. After housing: 60,000
2. After education: 75% of 60,000 = 45,000
3. After travel: 90% of 45,000 = ₹40,500

**Formula:** Remainder = Income × 0.80 × 0.75 × 0.90  
**Trap:** Each later rate applies to the shrinking remainder.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-010 · L3 · hard · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

Consider the following for a person with positive savings:

I. If income and expenditure both rise by the same percentage, savings rise by that percentage.
II. If expenditure rises by a larger percentage than income, savings must fall.
III. If savings rise by a larger percentage than income, expenditure must rise by a smaller percentage than income.

Which of these are correct?

- **A.** I and III only ✅
- **B.** I only  _(error: misses that III follows from S = I − E)_
- **C.** I, II and III  _(error: accepts II without testing)_
- **D.** I and II only  _(error: II fails: income 100, expenditure 50, +10% and +15% still raise savings)_

**Working**

1. I: S' = kI − kE = kS, true
2. II: I = 100, E = 50; income +10% (+10), expenditure +15% (+7.5) ⇒ savings rise by 2.5, so false
3. III: ΔE = ΔI − ΔS < aI − aS = aE, so expenditure grows by less than a%, true

**Formula:** ΔS = ΔI − ΔE  
**Trap:** A bigger percentage on a smaller base can still be a smaller absolute rise.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-011 · L3 · hard · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

Suresh saves 15% of his income. Next year his income rises by 20% and his expenditure by 25%. What percentage of the new income does he save?

- **A.** 8.33%  _(error: 25 points added to expenditure instead of 25%)_
- **B.** 13.75%  _(error: new savings measured on old income)_
- **C.** 10%  _(error: 15 + 20 − 25 applied to rates)_
- **D.** 11.46% ✅

**Working**

1. Income 100, expenditure 85
2. New income 120, new expenditure 85 × 1.25 = 106.25
3. New savings = 13.75; share = 13.75/120 = 11.46%

**Formula:** Savings rate = Savings ÷ Income  
**Trap:** Percent changes cannot be added to a share directly.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-012 · L3 · hard · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

A person's expenditure rises by 20% and his savings by 10%, so that his income rises by 16%. The ratio of his original expenditure to savings is:

- **A.** 3:2 ✅
- **B.** 2:3  _(error: ratio inverted)_
- **C.** 3:5  _(error: expenditure to income reported)_
- **D.** 2:1  _(error: ratio of the two growth rates taken)_

**Working**

1. 1.20E + 1.10S = 1.16(E + S)
2. 0.04E = 0.06S
3. E : S = 3 : 2

**Formula:** Weighted growth: income growth is the average of E and S growth weighted by their size  
**Trap:** Alligation: E share ↔ (16 − 10), S share ↔ (20 − 16).

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-013 · L3 · hard · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

Neha spends 60% of her income. Her income rises by 15%. If her savings in rupees are to stay the same, by what percentage can her expenditure rise?

- **A.** 25% ✅
- **B.** 9%  _(error: 15% of 60 read as a percentage)_
- **C.** 15%  _(error: same percentage as income)_
- **D.** 37.5%  _(error: increase in income divided by savings)_

**Working**

1. Income 100, expenditure 60, savings 40
2. New income 115, savings still 40 ⇒ expenditure 75
3. Rise = 15/60 = 25%

**Formula:** New expenditure = New income − Unchanged savings  
**Trap:** The whole ₹15 income gain goes to expenditure, whose base is 60.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-014 · L3 · hard · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

An employee saves 30% of his salary. His salary rises by 20%, while prices of everything he buys rise by 25% and he keeps buying the same quantities. His new savings as a percentage of new salary are:

- **A.** 35%  _(error: 30 + (25 − 20) applied)_
- **B.** 25%  _(error: 30 + 20 − 25 applied to rates)_
- **C.** 27.08% ✅
- **D.** 32.5%  _(error: new savings measured on old salary)_

**Working**

1. Salary 100, spending 70
2. New spending = 70 × 1.25 = 87.5; new salary 120
3. New savings = 32.5 ⇒ 32.5/120 = 27.08%

**Formula:** Same quantities ⇒ spending scales with prices  
**Trap:** Divide by the new salary, not the old one.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-015 · L2 · medium · Income, expenditure and savings · officer

A's income is 20% less than B's and A's expenditure is 25% less than B's. If B saves 20% of his income, what percentage of his income does A save?

- **A.** 25% ✅
- **B.** 75%  _(error: A's expenditure share reported)_
- **C.** 20%  _(error: B's rate reported)_
- **D.** 15%  _(error: rates netted: 20 − 5)_

**Working**

1. B: income 100, expenditure 80
2. A: income 80, expenditure 60
3. A saves 20/80 = 25%

**Formula:** Savings rate = (Income − Expenditure) ÷ Income  
**Trap:** A's base is his own income of 80.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-016 · L1 · easy · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · foundation

A's salary is 25% more than B's. By what percentage is B's salary less than A's?

- **A.** 80%  _(error: B as a percentage of A)_
- **B.** 25%  _(error: same percentage assumed both ways)_
- **C.** 20% ✅
- **D.** 33 1/3%  _(error: 25/75 used)_

**Working**

1. A = 125 when B = 100
2. Difference 25 on base 125 = 20%

**Formula:** Less-than % = r/(100 + r) × 100  
**Trap:** The base switches to A's salary.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-017 · L1 · easy · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · foundation

150 m is what percentage of 1.2 km?

- **A.** 8%  _(error: 1200 ÷ 150 read as a percentage)_
- **B.** 12.5% ✅
- **C.** 125%  _(error: unit conversion skipped: 150/1.2)_
- **D.** 1.25%  _(error: 1.2 km taken as 12,000 m)_

**Working**

1. 1.2 km = 1,200 m
2. 150/1,200 × 100 = 12.5%

**Formula:** % = part ÷ whole × 100 (same units)  
**Trap:** Convert units before dividing.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-018 · L1 · easy · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · foundation

If 35% of a number is 147, then 60% of the number is:

- **A.** 252 ✅
- **B.** 183.75  _(error: 25 points added as 25% of 147)_
- **C.** 420  _(error: the number itself)_
- **D.** 88.2  _(error: 60% taken of 147)_

**Working**

1. Number = 147 × 100/35 = 420
2. 60% of 420 = 252

**Formula:** x% of N ⇒ N = value × 100/x  
**Trap:** Or directly 147 × 60/35.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-019 · L2 · medium · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · foundation

A is 40% more than B, and C is 30% less than A. Compared with B, C is:

- **A.** 2% more  _(error: sign of the change reversed)_
- **B.** 10% more  _(error: percentages netted (40 − 30))_
- **C.** 10% less  _(error: 30 − 40 with base ignored)_
- **D.** 2% less ✅

**Working**

1. B = 100 ⇒ A = 140
2. C = 70% of 140 = 98
3. C is 2 less than 100, i.e. 2% less

**Formula:** Chain the multipliers: 1.4 × 0.7 = 0.98  
**Trap:** Percentages on different bases cannot be subtracted.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-020 · L2 · medium · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · foundation

If 20% of A equals 30% of B, then A is how much percent more than B?

- **A.** 33 1/3%  _(error: B less than A reported)_
- **B.** 150%  _(error: A as a percentage of B)_
- **C.** 50% ✅
- **D.** 10%  _(error: difference of percentages)_

**Working**

1. 0.2A = 0.3B ⇒ A = 1.5B
2. A exceeds B by 50%

**Formula:** A/B = 30/20  
**Trap:** 'More than' needs (A − B)/B.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-021 · L3 · hard · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

A's income is 20% more than B's, and B's income is 25% less than C's. By what percentage is C's income more than A's?

- **A.** 11 1/9% ✅
- **B.** 10%  _(error: A less than C reported (base C))_
- **C.** 5%  _(error: rates netted: 25 − 20)_
- **D.** 111 1/9%  _(error: C as a percentage of A)_

**Working**

1. C = 100 ⇒ B = 75 ⇒ A = 90
2. C exceeds A by 10 on base 90 = 11 1/9%

**Formula:** More-than % uses the smaller quantity as base  
**Trap:** 10% less the other way is 11 1/9% more.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-022 · L3 · hard · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

A number exceeds 15% of itself by 1,020. What is 35% of the number?

- **A.** 357  _(error: excess treated as the number)_
- **B.** 410.55  _(error: number taken as 1.15 × 1,020)_
- **C.** 420 ✅
- **D.** 2,380  _(error: 1,020 taken as 15% of the number)_

**Working**

1. N − 0.15N = 1,020 ⇒ 0.85N = 1,020 ⇒ N = 1,200
2. 35% of 1,200 = 420

**Formula:** N(1 − 0.15) = excess  
**Trap:** The excess is 85% of the number.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-023 · L3 · hard · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

Two numbers are respectively 30% and 45% more than a third number. The first number is what percentage of the second?

- **A.** 85%  _(error: 100 − (45 − 30))_
- **B.** 11.54%  _(error: gap measured on first number)_
- **C.** 89.66% ✅
- **D.** 111.54%  _(error: second as a percentage of first)_

**Working**

1. Third = 100 ⇒ numbers 130 and 145
2. 130/145 × 100 = 89.66%

**Formula:** Ratio of (100 + a) to (100 + b)  
**Trap:** Compare the two numbers, not their markups.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-024 · L3 · hard · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

In an examination, 65% of candidates passed in English, 58% passed in Mathematics and 12% failed in both. What percentage passed in both?

- **A.** 88%  _(error: everyone except the both-fail group)_
- **B.** 35% ✅
- **C.** 53%  _(error: only English failures and both-failures removed)_
- **D.** 23%  _(error: failed-in-both group ignored)_

**Working**

1. Failed English 35%, failed Maths 42%
2. Failed at least one = 35 + 42 − 12 = 65%
3. Passed both = 100 − 65 = 35%

**Formula:** n(A ∪ B) = n(A) + n(B) − n(A ∩ B)  
**Trap:** Work with failure sets and use inclusion–exclusion.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-025 · L3 · hard · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

A scored 30% fewer marks than B, and B scored 20% more than C. If A scored 336, C scored:

- **A.** 400 ✅
- **B.** 384  _(error: '20% more' reversed as 20% less of B)_
- **C.** 280  _(error: 30% step skipped)_
- **D.** 480  _(error: B's score reported)_

**Working**

1. B = 336/0.7 = 480
2. C = 480/1.2 = 400

**Formula:** Undo each change by division  
**Trap:** C = B/1.2, not 0.8B.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-026 · L3 · hard · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

The price of an item rises from ₹80 to ₹100. Consider:

I. The increase is 25%.
II. To return to ₹80, the new price must fall by 20%.
III. The old price is 75% of the new price.

Which are correct?

- **A.** I and II only ✅
- **B.** I and III only  _(error: III: 80/100 = 80%, not 75%)_
- **C.** II only  _(error: I wrongly rejected)_
- **D.** I, II and III  _(error: III accepted by symmetry)_

**Working**

1. Increase = 20/80 = 25% (I true)
2. Decrease = 20/100 = 20% (II true)
3. 80/100 = 80% (III false)

**Formula:** Change % = change ÷ original  
**Trap:** Rise and fall percentages differ because the base differs.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-027 · L2 · medium · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

If A is 3/5 of B, then B is how much percent more than A?

- **A.** 60%  _(error: A as a percentage of B)_
- **B.** 66 2/3% ✅
- **C.** 40%  _(error: A less than B reported)_
- **D.** 166 2/3%  _(error: B as a percentage of A)_

**Working**

1. A = 3, B = 5
2. B exceeds A by 2 on base 3 = 66 2/3%

**Formula:** (B − A)/A × 100  
**Trap:** Base is A.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-028 · L3 · hard · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

In a two-candidate election, 12% of voters did not vote and 5% of the votes cast were invalid. The winner got 55% of the valid votes and won by 2,090 votes. The total number of voters was:

- **A.** 23,750  _(error: invalid votes ignored)_
- **B.** 25,000 ✅
- **C.** 22,000  _(error: non-voters ignored)_
- **D.** 50,000  _(error: margin taken as 5% instead of 10% of valid votes)_

**Working**

1. Votes cast = 0.88N; valid = 0.95 × 0.88N = 0.836N
2. Margin = (55 − 45)% of valid = 0.0836N
3. 0.0836N = 2,090 ⇒ N = 25,000

**Formula:** Margin = (winner% − loser%) × valid votes  
**Trap:** Two layers of shrinkage before the margin.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-029 · L3 · hard · Percentage comparison — what percent of / more than · officer

A pen costs 60% of a notebook, and a notebook costs 25% more than a file. By what percentage is the file costlier than the pen?

- **A.** 25%  _(error: pen cheaper than file reported)_
- **B.** 33 1/3% ✅
- **C.** 75%  _(error: pen as a percentage of file)_
- **D.** 15%  _(error: rates netted: 40 − 25)_

**Working**

1. File = 100 ⇒ notebook 125 ⇒ pen 75
2. File exceeds pen by 25 on base 75 = 33 1/3%

**Formula:** Chain: pen = 0.6 × 1.25 × file  
**Trap:** The base for 'costlier than the pen' is the pen.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-030 · L1 · easy · Percentage increase and decrease · foundation

The price of a gas cylinder rises from ₹240 to ₹282. The percentage increase is:

- **A.** 17.5% ✅
- **B.** 42%  _(error: absolute rise read as a percentage)_
- **C.** 14.89%  _(error: increase divided by new price)_
- **D.** 1.75%  _(error: decimal slip)_

**Working**

1. Rise = 282 − 240 = 42
2. 42/240 × 100 = 17.5%

**Formula:** % increase = rise ÷ original × 100  
**Trap:** Base is the original price.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-031 · L1 · easy · Percentage increase and decrease · foundation

The price of sugar falls by 20%. By what percentage must a household increase consumption to keep its expenditure unchanged?

- **A.** 80%  _(error: new price ratio reported)_
- **B.** 25% ✅
- **C.** 20%  _(error: same percentage assumed)_
- **D.** 16 2/3%  _(error: 20/120 used)_

**Working**

1. Price 100 → 80
2. Consumption must rise 100/80 = 1.25 ⇒ 25%

**Formula:** Required % = r/(100 − r) × 100  
**Trap:** Expenditure = price × quantity must stay constant.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-032 · L2 · medium · Percentage increase and decrease · foundation

The price of rice rises by 25%. A family reduces consumption so that its expenditure on rice rises by only 10%. The percentage reduction in consumption is:

- **A.** 8.8%  _(error: new consumption ratio 0.88 misread as 8.8%)_
- **B.** 20%  _(error: full compensation for 25% rise)_
- **C.** 12% ✅
- **D.** 15%  _(error: rates subtracted: 25 − 10)_

**Working**

1. New expenditure/Old = 1.10 = 1.25 × q
2. q = 1.10/1.25 = 0.88
3. Reduction = 12%

**Formula:** Expenditure = Price × Quantity  
**Trap:** Divide the multipliers, do not subtract percentages.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-033 · L1 · easy · Percentage increase and decrease · foundation

A 20% reduction in the price of apples lets a buyer get 4 kg more for ₹800. The original price per kg was:

- **A.** ₹160  _(error: money saved reported)_
- **B.** ₹200  _(error: ₹800 divided by 4 kg)_
- **C.** ₹40  _(error: reduced price reported)_
- **D.** ₹50 ✅

**Working**

1. Saving = 20% of 800 = ₹160, which buys 4 kg
2. Reduced price = ₹40/kg
3. Original = 40/0.8 = ₹50/kg

**Formula:** Saving ÷ extra quantity = reduced price  
**Trap:** The ₹160 buys the extra kg at the reduced price.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-034 · L2 · medium · Percentage increase and decrease · foundation

A number increased by 35% becomes 945. If the same number had instead been decreased by 35%, the result would be:

- **A.** 245  _(error: 35% of the original reported)_
- **B.** 455 ✅
- **C.** 283.5  _(error: 70% of 945 subtracted)_
- **D.** 614.25  _(error: 35% deducted from 945 instead of the original)_

**Working**

1. Original = 945/1.35 = 700
2. Decreased value = 0.65 × 700 = 455

**Formula:** Original = New ÷ (1 + r)  
**Trap:** Recover the original first.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-035 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

Petrol prices rise by 30%. A motorist wants to limit the rise in his fuel bill to 17%. By what percentage must he cut consumption?

- **A.** 23.08%  _(error: full compensation for price rise)_
- **B.** 13%  _(error: rates subtracted)_
- **C.** 11.11%  _(error: gap divided by 117 instead of 130)_
- **D.** 10% ✅

**Working**

1. 1.17 = 1.30 × q
2. q = 0.90
3. Cut = 10%

**Formula:** q = (1 + e)/(1 + p)  
**Trap:** Divide new expenditure multiplier by price multiplier.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-036 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

A 12.5% fall in the price of dal enables a buyer to purchase 3 kg more for ₹1,344. The original price per kg was:

- **A.** ₹49  _(error: reduction applied twice)_
- **B.** ₹64 ✅
- **C.** ₹56  _(error: reduced price reported)_
- **D.** ₹448  _(error: total divided by extra quantity)_

**Working**

1. Saving = 12.5% of 1,344 = ₹168 buys 3 kg ⇒ reduced price ₹56
2. Original = 56 ÷ 0.875 = ₹64

**Formula:** Reduced price = saving ÷ extra kg  
**Trap:** Scale back up to get the original.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-037 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

An employee's pay is cut by 20% and later raised by 20%. By what percentage must it now be raised to restore the original pay?

- **A.** 4 1/6% ✅
- **B.** 4%  _(error: net loss 4% taken as needed rise)_
- **C.** 0%  _(error: believes cut and raise cancel)_
- **D.** 20%  _(error: raise equal to cut assumed)_

**Working**

1. 100 → 80 → 96
2. Needed rise = 4/96 = 4 1/6%

**Formula:** Required % = loss/remaining × 100  
**Trap:** The base is 96, not 100.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-038 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

A museum cuts its ticket price by 25% and the number of visitors rises by 60%. The effect on ticket revenue is:

- **A.** 35% increase  _(error: percentages added)_
- **B.** 20% increase ✅
- **C.** 15% increase  _(error: cross term 15% taken as net effect)_
- **D.** 20% decrease  _(error: sign reversed)_

**Working**

1. Revenue multiplier = 0.75 × 1.60 = 1.20
2. Increase = 20%

**Formula:** Revenue = Price × Volume  
**Trap:** Net % = a + b + ab/100 = 60 − 25 − 15.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-039 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

Water expands by 10% in volume on freezing. By what percentage does ice shrink on melting back to water?

- **A.** 10%  _(error: same percentage assumed)_
- **B.** 9%  _(error: decimal truncated)_
- **C.** 11%  _(error: rough reciprocal)_
- **D.** 9 1/11% ✅

**Working**

1. Water 100 → ice 110
2. Shrink 10 on base 110 = 9 1/11%

**Formula:** r/(100 + r) × 100  
**Trap:** Base is the ice volume.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-040 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

A student multiplied a number by 3/5 instead of 5/3. The percentage error in the result is:

- **A.** 40%  _(error: (5 − 3)/5 used)_
- **B.** 36%  _(error: ratio of results taken as the error)_
- **C.** 177.78%  _(error: error measured on the wrong result)_
- **D.** 64% ✅

**Working**

1. Correct = 5N/3, obtained = 3N/5
2. Error = 16N/15
3. % error = (16/15)/(5/3) × 100 = 64%

**Formula:** % error = |correct − obtained| ÷ correct × 100  
**Trap:** Measure against the correct value.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-041 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

Because of a 16 2/3% fall in the price of mangoes, a buyer gets 5 more mangoes for ₹120. The original price per mango was:

- **A.** ₹4.80 ✅
- **B.** ₹3.33  _(error: reduction applied twice)_
- **C.** ₹24  _(error: ₹120 ÷ 5)_
- **D.** ₹4  _(error: reduced price reported)_

**Working**

1. Saving = 1/6 of 120 = ₹20 buys 5 mangoes ⇒ reduced price ₹4
2. Original = 4 × 6/5 = ₹4.80

**Formula:** Reduced price = saving ÷ extra count  
**Trap:** 16 2/3% = 1/6.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-042 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

In a school, the number of boys increases by 20% and the number of girls by 30%, so that total strength rises by 24%. The original ratio of boys to girls was:

- **A.** 2:5  _(error: girls to total reported)_
- **B.** 2:3  _(error: alligation sides swapped)_
- **C.** 3:2 ✅
- **D.** 3:5  _(error: boys to total reported)_

**Working**

1. 20b + 30g = 24(b + g)
2. 6g = 4b ⇒ b : g = 3 : 2

**Formula:** Alligation on percentage growth  
**Trap:** The group nearer the average growth is larger.

## QAA-043 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

A shop's revenue rose from ₹4.8 lakh to ₹5.4 lakh while units sold fell by 10%. The percentage change in average price per unit is:

- **A.** 12.5% increase  _(error: revenue change taken as price change)_
- **B.** 2.5% increase  _(error: 12.5% − 10%)_
- **C.** 22.5% increase  _(error: 12.5% + 10% added)_
- **D.** 25% increase ✅

**Working**

1. Revenue multiplier = 5.4/4.8 = 1.125
2. Price multiplier = 1.125/0.9 = 1.25

**Formula:** Price = Revenue ÷ Volume  
**Trap:** Divide multipliers.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-044 · L3 · hard · Percentage increase and decrease · officer

X earns 40% more than Y. Y gets a 50% raise and X a 20% raise. Now:

- **A.** X earns 10.71% more than Y  _(error: gap measured on X's new salary)_
- **B.** X earns 10% more than Y  _(error: raises netted: 40 − 30)_
- **C.** X earns 12% more than Y ✅
- **D.** Y earns 12% more than X  _(error: direction reversed)_

**Working**

1. Y = 100 → 150; X = 140 → 168
2. X exceeds Y by 18 on 150 = 12%

**Formula:** Apply each raise to its own base  
**Trap:** Compare on Y's new salary.

## QAA-045 · L1 · easy · Population, strength and quantity change · foundation

A town's population of 50,000 grows at 8% per annum. Its population after 2 years will be:

- **A.** 54,000  _(error: only one year applied)_
- **B.** 58,000  _(error: simple growth of 16%)_
- **C.** 58,320 ✅
- **D.** 42,320  _(error: decline instead of growth)_

**Working**

1. 50,000 × 1.08 = 54,000
2. 54,000 × 1.08 = 58,320

**Formula:** P(1 + r)^n  
**Trap:** Growth compounds on the new base.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-046 · L1 · easy · Population, strength and quantity change · foundation

A machine loses 10% of its value every year. After 3 years it is worth ₹72,900. Its original value was:

- **A.** ₹97,029.90  _(error: 10% added back each year)_
- **B.** ₹1,00,000 ✅
- **C.** ₹1,04,142.86  _(error: simple 30% depreciation)_
- **D.** ₹90,000  _(error: only two years undone)_

**Working**

1. Original × 0.9³ = 72,900
2. Original = 72,900/0.729 = 1,00,000

**Formula:** V = V0(1 − r)^n  
**Trap:** Adding 10% back is not the inverse of losing 10%.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-047 · L2 · medium · Population, strength and quantity change · foundation

A village of 20,000 people grows by 10% in the first year and declines by 10% in the second. The population after 2 years is:

- **A.** 20,000  _(error: assumes +10% and −10% cancel)_
- **B.** 20,200  _(error: net 1% taken as gain)_
- **C.** 19,000  _(error: decline applied to original twice)_
- **D.** 19,800 ✅

**Working**

1. 20,000 × 1.1 = 22,000
2. 22,000 × 0.9 = 19,800

**Formula:** Successive multipliers 1.1 × 0.9 = 0.99  
**Trap:** Equal up and down moves give a net fall.

## QAA-048 · L2 · medium · Population, strength and quantity change · foundation

A school's enrolment rose by 15% to 1,610. The earlier enrolment was:

- **A.** 1,400 ✅
- **B.** 1,851.5  _(error: multiplied instead of divided)_
- **C.** 1,595  _(error: 15 subtracted)_
- **D.** 1,368.5  _(error: 15% deducted from the new figure)_

**Working**

1. Earlier × 1.15 = 1,610
2. Earlier = 1,400

**Formula:** Old = New ÷ (1 + r)  
**Trap:** 15% of 1,610 is not 15% of the old value.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-049 · L2 · medium · Population, strength and quantity change · foundation

A village has 12,000 people, 55% of them male. Next year males increase by 10% and females by 20%. The new population is:

- **A.** 12,660  _(error: female growth left out)_
- **B.** 13,740 ✅
- **C.** 13,800  _(error: average 15% applied to total)_
- **D.** 13,860  _(error: rates swapped between males and females)_

**Working**

1. Males 6,600 → 7,260; females 5,400 → 6,480
2. Total = 13,740

**Formula:** Grow each group separately  
**Trap:** Weights are 55:45, not 50:50.

## QAA-050 · L3 · hard · Population, strength and quantity change · officer

A city of 2,50,000 grows 5% in year 1 and 8% in year 2, then declines 4% in year 3. The population at the end of year 3 is:

- **A.** 2,83,500  _(error: year 3 ignored)_
- **B.** 2,94,840  _(error: decline treated as growth)_
- **C.** 2,72,160 ✅
- **D.** 2,72,500  _(error: rates added: 5 + 8 − 4)_

**Working**

1. 2,50,000 × 1.05 = 2,62,500
2. × 1.08 = 2,83,500
3. × 0.96 = 2,72,160

**Formula:** P × Π(1 ± r_i)  
**Trap:** Each year's change acts on the previous year's figure.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-051 · L3 · hard · Population, strength and quantity change · officer

A town of 50,000 grows by 10% a year, but 2,000 people move out at the end of each year. The population after 2 years is:

- **A.** 56,000  _(error: simple 20% growth then 4,000 removed)_
- **B.** 56,300 ✅
- **C.** 56,500  _(error: compound growth, then all migration removed at the end)_
- **D.** 55,880  _(error: migration deducted before growth)_

**Working**

1. Year 1: 55,000 − 2,000 = 53,000
2. Year 2: 58,300 − 2,000 = 56,300

**Formula:** P_next = P × 1.1 − 2,000  
**Trap:** Year-2 growth applies after year-1 migration.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-052 · L3 · hard · Population, strength and quantity change · officer

A bacterial culture grows by 25% every hour. After 2 hours it has 22,500 cells. The initial count was:

- **A.** 12,656.25  _(error: 25% removed twice)_
- **B.** 15,000  _(error: simple 50% growth)_
- **C.** 16,875  _(error: 25% removed once)_
- **D.** 14,400 ✅

**Working**

1. Initial × 1.25² = 22,500
2. Initial = 22,500/1.5625 = 14,400

**Formula:** N0 = N ÷ (1 + r)^t  
**Trap:** Reverse growth by dividing, not by subtracting 25%.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-053 · L3 · hard · Population, strength and quantity change · officer

A machine bought for ₹5,00,000 depreciates 20% a year for two years and 10% in the third year (reducing balance). The total loss in value over 3 years is:

- **A.** ₹2,12,000 ✅
- **B.** ₹2,88,000  _(error: value after 3 years reported, not the loss)_
- **C.** ₹2,30,000  _(error: third-year 10% taken on original cost)_
- **D.** ₹2,50,000  _(error: rates added: 20 + 20 + 10)_

**Working**

1. 5,00,000 × 0.8 × 0.8 = 3,20,000
2. × 0.9 = 2,88,000
3. Loss = 2,12,000

**Formula:** Loss = V0 − V0 × Π(1 − r)  
**Trap:** Reducing balance: each rate applies to the written-down value.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-054 · L3 · hard · Population, strength and quantity change · officer

In a college, the number of boys rises by 20% and the number of girls falls by 10%, leaving total strength of 1,800 unchanged. The number of boys now is:

- **A.** 720 ✅
- **B.** 1,200  _(error: original girls reported)_
- **C.** 1,080  _(error: girls now reported)_
- **D.** 600  _(error: original number of boys)_

**Working**

1. 0.2B = 0.1G ⇒ G = 2B
2. B = 600, G = 1,200
3. Boys now = 720

**Formula:** Gains and losses must balance when the total is unchanged  
**Trap:** Ratio from 20B = 10G.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-055 · L3 · hard · Population, strength and quantity change · officer

Over three years a district's population rose 10%, then 20%, then fell 10%, reaching 2,37,600. The population at the start was:

- **A.** 1,98,000  _(error: net +20% (rates added))_
- **B.** 2,16,000  _(error: only the first year undone)_
- **C.** 1,63,636.36  _(error: the 10% fall treated as a rise)_
- **D.** 2,00,000 ✅

**Working**

1. Multiplier = 1.1 × 1.2 × 0.9 = 1.188
2. Start = 2,37,600/1.188 = 2,00,000

**Formula:** P0 = P3 ÷ Π(1 ± r)  
**Trap:** Undo all three years together.

## QAA-056 · L3 · hard · Population, strength and quantity change · officer

City A (1,00,000) grows 10% a year; city B (1,50,000) shrinks 10% a year. After 2 years:

- **A.** B exceeds A by 1,500  _(error: A grown simply, B shrunk with compounding)_
- **B.** Both are equal  _(error: simple (non-compound) change used)_

- **D.** B exceeds A by 500 ✅

**Working**

1. A = 1,00,000 × 1.21 = 1,21,000

## QAA-059 · L3 · hard · Population, strength and quantity change · officer

## QAA-067 · L3 · hard · Successive percentage change · officer

## QAA-068 · L3 · hard · Successive percentage change · officer

**Working**

1. Stage 1: 1.2 × 0.8 = 0.96
2. Stage 2: 0.9 × 1.1 = 0.99
3. Net: 0.9504 ⇒ 4.96% decrease

## QAA-069 · L3 · hard · Successive percentage change · officer

A wage is cut by 10%, then raised by 10%, then cut again by 10%. The overall change is:

- **A.** 10% decrease  _(error: percentages added)_
- **B.** 10.9% decrease ✅
- **C.** 11% decrease  _(error: first two steps −1%, then −10% added)_
- **D.** 8.91% decrease  _(error: factor 0.891 misread)_

**Working**

1. 0.9 × 1.1 × 0.9 = 0.891
2. Decrease 10.9%

**Formula:** Multiply all factors  
**Trap:** Additive shortcuts fail across three steps.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-070 · L3 · hard · Successive percentage change · officer

Three successive increases of 10%, 20% and 30% are equivalent to a single increase of:

- **A.** 62%  _(error: first two compounded, third added)_
- **B.** 60%  _(error: rates added)_
- **C.** 56%  _(error: first increase missed)_
- **D.** 71.6% ✅

**Working**

1. 1.1 × 1.2 = 1.32
2. 1.32 × 1.3 = 1.716 ⇒ 71.6%

**Formula:** Π(1 + r) − 1  
**Trap:** Three-way compounding adds more than the pairwise cross terms.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-071 · L3 · hard · Successive percentage change · officer

A number is increased by 30% and the result decreased by 30%. The final value is 2,700 less than the original. The original number is:

- **A.** 30,000 ✅
- **B.** 2,70,000  _(error: net loss taken as 1%)_
- **C.** 2,967.03  _(error: divided by final factor 0.91)_
- **D.** 12,857.14  _(error: 0.3 × 0.7 used as loss)_

**Working**

1. 1.3 × 0.7 = 0.91 ⇒ loss 9%
2. 0.09N = 2,700 ⇒ N = 30,000

**Formula:** Loss = x²/100 %  
**Trap:** The 9% loss is on the original.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-072 · L3 · hard · Successive percentage change · officer

A box's length is increased by 25% and breadth by 20%. By what percentage must its height be reduced to keep the volume unchanged?

- **A.** 33 1/3% ✅
- **B.** 45%  _(error: rates added)_
- **C.** 50%  _(error: combined 50% increase taken as the cut)_
- **D.** 30%  _(error: net of 45 and cross term misapplied)_

**Working**

1. 1.25 × 1.2 = 1.5
2. Height factor = 1/1.5 = 2/3
3. Reduction = 33 1/3%

**Formula:** Required cut = (F − 1)/F  
**Trap:** Cut = 50/150, not 50%.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-073 · L3 · hard · Successive percentage change · officer

A price rises by 10% each year for two years and then falls by 21%. Compared with the start, the price is now:

- **A.** 1% lower  _(error: 21 − 20 netted)_
- **B.** 4.41% lower ✅
- **C.** unchanged  _(error: 21% rise and 21% fall assumed to cancel)_
- **D.** 4.41% higher  _(error: sign reversed)_

**Working**

1. 1.1² = 1.21
2. 1.21 × 0.79 = 0.9559
3. 4.41% lower

**Formula:** Multiply factors  
**Trap:** Equal percentage up and down leaves a net fall.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-074 · L3 · hard · Successive percentage change · officer

Two successive discounts of 15% and d% equal a single discount of 32%. Then d is:

- **A.** 17%  _(error: 32 − 15 subtracted)_
- **B.** 47%  _(error: discounts added)_
- **C.** 25%  _(error: gap divided by final price)_
- **D.** 20% ✅

**Working**

1. 0.85 × (1 − d) = 0.68
2. 1 − d = 0.8 ⇒ d = 20%

**Formula:** Π(1 − d) = 1 − D  
**Trap:** Second discount acts on 85, so 17/85.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-075 · L1 · easy · Cost price, selling price and margin · foundation

An article bought for ₹640 is sold for ₹736. The profit percentage is:

- **A.** 115%  _(error: SP as a percentage of CP)_
- **B.** 13.04%  _(error: profit divided by selling price)_
- **C.** 15% ✅
- **D.** 96%  _(error: profit amount read as a percentage)_

**Working**

1. Profit = 736 − 640 = 96
2. 96/640 × 100 = 15%

**Formula:** Profit % = Profit ÷ CP × 100  
**Trap:** Profit % is on cost unless stated otherwise.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-076 · L1 · easy · Cost price, selling price and margin · foundation

A chair costing ₹1,250 is sold at a loss of 12%. Its selling price is:

- **A.** ₹150  _(error: loss amount reported)_
- **B.** ₹1,400  _(error: loss treated as profit)_
- **C.** ₹1,100 ✅
- **D.** ₹1,116.07  _(error: divided by 1.12)_

**Working**

1. SP = 1,250 × 0.88 = 1,100

**Formula:** SP = CP × (1 − loss%)  
**Trap:** Loss reduces the cost by 12%.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-077 · L2 · medium · Cost price, selling price and margin · foundation

Selling a watch for ₹1,008 gives a profit of 12%. To earn 20% profit, it should be sold for:

- **A.** ₹840  _(error: divided by 1.2)_
- **B.** ₹1,080 ✅
- **C.** ₹1,209.60  _(error: 20% applied on the old SP)_
- **D.** ₹1,088.64  _(error: extra 8% applied on the old SP)_

**Working**

1. CP = 1,008/1.12 = 900
2. SP = 900 × 1.2 = 1,080

**Formula:** Find CP first, then apply new margin  
**Trap:** Both margins are on cost.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-078 · L2 · medium · Cost price, selling price and margin · foundation

The selling price of 12 articles equals the cost price of 15 articles. The profit percentage is:

- **A.** 20%  _(error: gain divided by 15)_
- **B.** 3%  _(error: difference in counts read as %)_
- **C.** 80%  _(error: 12/15 reported)_
- **D.** 25% ✅

**Working**

1. 12 SP = 15 CP ⇒ SP/CP = 15/12 = 1.25
2. Profit = 25%

**Formula:** Profit % = (CP-count − SP-count)/SP-count × 100  
**Trap:** Divide by the smaller (SP) count.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-079 · L2 · medium · Cost price, selling price and margin · foundation

A trader claims to sell at a profit of 20%, but uses a 900 g weight in place of 1 kg. His actual profit percentage is:

- **A.** 8%  _(error: 1.2 × 0.9 − 1)_
- **B.** 30%  _(error: 20% + 10% added)_
- **C.** 33 1/3% ✅
- **D.** 20%  _(error: false weight ignored)_

**Working**

1. Customer pays for 1,000 g at 1.2 × cost, receives 900 g
2. Gain factor = 1.2/0.9 = 4/3
3. Profit = 33 1/3%

**Formula:** Profit factor = (1 + p) × true/false weight  
**Trap:** Divide by 0.9, do not add 10%.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-080 · L3 · hard · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

The profit on selling an article for ₹1,480 is three times the loss on selling it for ₹1,160. The cost price is:

- **A.** ₹1,240 ✅
- **B.** ₹1,320  _(error: profit taken equal to loss (midpoint))_
- **C.** ₹1,400  _(error: factor 3 put on the profit side)_
- **D.** ₹1,266.67  _(error: gap split in 1 : 2 instead of 1 : 3)_

**Working**

1. 1,480 − C = 3(C − 1,160)
2. 4C = 1,480 + 3,480 = 4,960
3. C = 1,240

**Formula:** Profit = SP − CP; Loss = CP − SP  
**Trap:** The cost lies 1/4 of the way from 1,160 to 1,480.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-081 · L3 · hard · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

A trader's margin is 20% of the selling price. If his cost rises by 10% and the selling price is unchanged, his new margin as a percentage of the selling price is:

- **A.** 13.64%  _(error: new margin measured on cost)_
- **B.** 10%  _(error: margin cut point-for-point)_
- **C.** 18%  _(error: 10% taken of the margin)_
- **D.** 12% ✅

**Working**

1. SP 100, CP 80
2. New CP = 88
3. Margin = 12/100 = 12%

**Formula:** Margin on SP = (SP − CP)/SP  
**Trap:** A 10% cost rise is 8 points of SP here.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-082 · L3 · hard · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

A vendor buys oranges at 5 for ₹12 and sells them at 4 for ₹11. His profit percentage is:

- **A.** 8 1/3%  _(error: (12 − 11)/12 used)_
- **B.** 12 8/11%  _(error: profit on SP)_
- **C.** 14 7/12% ✅
- **D.** 25%  _(error: counts 5 and 4 compared only)_

**Working**

1. CP each = 12/5 = 2.40
2. SP each = 11/4 = 2.75
3. Profit = 0.35/2.40 = 14 7/12%

**Formula:** Compare per-unit CP and SP  
**Trap:** Convert both rates to one orange.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-083 · L3 · hard · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

By selling 33 m of cloth, a shopkeeper gains the selling price of 11 m. His gain percentage is:

- **A.** 33 1/3%  _(error: gain measured on SP)_
- **B.** 66 2/3%  _(error: 22/33 used)_
- **C.** 50% ✅
- **D.** 25%  _(error: 11/(33 + 11) used)_

**Working**

1. 33 SP − 33 CP = 11 SP ⇒ 22 SP = 33 CP
2. SP/CP = 1.5 ⇒ gain 50%

**Formula:** Gain = SP of 11 m ⇒ cost of 33 m = SP of 22 m  
**Trap:** Gain % is on cost.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-084 · L3 · hard · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

A man sells a cycle at 10% profit. Had he bought it 10% cheaper and sold it for ₹30 more, he would have gained 25%. The cost price is:

- **A.** ₹600  _(error: 1.25 × 0.9 taken as 1.15)_
- **B.** ₹200  _(error: cheaper purchase ignored)_
- **C.** ₹1,500  _(error: ₹30 treated as 2% of cost)_
- **D.** ₹1,200 ✅

**Working**

1. Actual SP = 1.1C
2. New CP = 0.9C; new SP = 1.25 × 0.9C = 1.125C
3. 1.125C − 1.1C = 30 ⇒ C = 1,200

**Formula:** Equate the two SP expressions  
**Trap:** The 25% gain is on the reduced cost.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-085 · L3 · hard · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

A manufacturer sells to a dealer at 20% profit and the dealer sells to a customer at 25% profit. The customer pays ₹2,250. The manufacturer's cost is:

- **A.** ₹1,237.50  _(error: 45% deducted from customer price)_
- **B.** ₹1,500 ✅
- **C.** ₹1,687.50  _(error: 25% deducted from customer price)_
- **D.** ₹1,551.72  _(error: margins added (45%))_

**Working**

1. 2,250 = C × 1.2 × 1.25 = 1.5C
2. C = 1,500

**Formula:** Chain the markups  
**Trap:** Divide by the product of factors.

## QAA-086 · L3 · hard · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

A product's cost is 45% materials, 35% labour and 20% overheads. Material prices rise 20% and wages 10%; overheads are unchanged. To keep the same profit percentage, the selling price must rise by:

- **A.** 12.5% ✅
- **B.** 15.62%  _(error: cost rise grossed up by a 25% margin)_
- **C.** 15%  _(error: average of 20% and 10%)_
- **D.** 30%  _(error: rises added)_

**Working**

1. Cost rise = 0.45 × 20 + 0.35 × 10 = 9 + 3.5 = 12.5%
2. Same profit % ⇒ SP rises by the same 12.5%

**Formula:** Weighted rise = Σ share × rise  
**Trap:** Weight each rise by its cost share.

## QAA-087 · L3 · hard · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

A sells a bicycle to B at a 15% loss; B sells it to C at a 20% profit. C pays ₹8,160. A's cost price was:

- **A.** ₹6,800  _(error: B's cost reported)_
- **B.** ₹8,000 ✅
- **C.** ₹7,771.43  _(error: net +5% (rates added))_
- **D.** ₹8,323.20  _(error: net factor multiplied)_

**Working**

1. Net factor = 0.85 × 1.2 = 1.02
2. A's cost = 8,160/1.02 = 8,000

**Formula:** CP = Final price ÷ Π factors  
**Trap:** Loss then gain: multiply 0.85 × 1.2.

## QAA-088 · L2 · medium · Cost price, selling price and margin · officer

The cost price of 25 pens equals the selling price of x pens. If the profit is 25%, x equals:

- **A.** 20 ✅
- **B.** 31.25  _(error: 25 multiplied by 1.25)_
- **C.** 30  _(error: 5 pens added for 25%)_
- **D.** 18.75  _(error: 25 reduced by 25%)_

**Working**

1. 25 CP = x × 1.25 CP
2. x = 25/1.25 = 20

**Formula:** x = n ÷ (1 + p)  
**Trap:** Divide, since each SP is larger than CP.

## QAA-089 · L1 · easy · Discount and marked price · foundation

A jacket is marked ₹2,400 and sold at a 15% discount. The selling price is:

- **A.** ₹360  _(error: discount amount reported)_
- **B.** ₹2,086.96  _(error: MP divided by 1.15)_
- **C.** ₹2,040 ✅
- **D.** ₹2,760  _(error: 15% added)_

**Working**

1. Discount = 15% of 2,400 = 360
2. SP = 2,040

**Formula:** SP = MP × (1 − d)  
**Trap:** Discount is on marked price.

## QAA-090 · L1 · easy · Discount and marked price · foundation

A shopkeeper marks goods 25% above cost and allows a 12% discount. His profit percentage is:

- **A.** 13%  _(error: percentages subtracted)_
- **B.** 10% ✅
- **C.** 12%  _(error: discount read as profit)_
- **D.** 10.4%  _(error: gap measured on MP)_

**Working**

1. CP 100 ⇒ MP 125
2. SP = 125 × 0.88 = 110
3. Profit = 10%

**Formula:** SP = CP × (1 + m)(1 − d)  
**Trap:** Discount acts on the marked price.

## QAA-091 · L2 · medium · Discount and marked price · foundation

After a 15% discount, a phone sells for ₹3,570. Its marked price is:

- **A.** ₹4,105.50  _(error: 15% of SP added)_
- **B.** ₹535.50  _(error: discount on SP reported)_
- **C.** ₹4,200 ✅
- **D.** ₹3,034.50  _(error: 15% deducted again)_

**Working**

1. MP × 0.85 = 3,570
2. MP = 4,200

**Formula:** MP = SP ÷ (1 − d)

## QAA-092 · L2 · medium · Discount and marked price · foundation

A trader gives a 20% discount and still makes a 20% profit. By what percentage is the marked price above cost?

- **A.** 44%  _(error: 1.2 × 1.2 − 1)_
- **B.** 50% ✅
- **C.** 40%  _(error: percentages added)_
- **D.** 25%  _(error: 20/80 only)_

**Working**

1. MP × 0.8 = 1.2 CP
2. MP = 1.5 CP ⇒ 50% above cost

**Formula:** MP/CP = (1 + p)/(1 − d)  
**Trap:** Divide by 0.8.

## QAA-093 · L2 · medium · Discount and marked price · foundation

A 'buy 3, get 1 free' offer is equivalent to a discount of:

- **A.** 33 1/3%  _(error: free item divided by items paid for)_
- **B.** 20%  _(error: buy 4, get 1 free)_
- **C.** 25% ✅
- **D.** 75%  _(error: share paid for)_

**Working**

1. Customer pays for 3, gets 4
2. Discount = 1/4 = 25%

**Formula:** Discount = free ÷ total received

## QAA-094 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

A trader marks goods 40% above cost and allows successive discounts of 10% and 5%. His profit percentage is:

- **A.** 25%  _(error: discounts subtracted from markup)_
- **B.** 19.7% ✅
- **C.** 19%  _(error: discounts added into 15%)_
- **D.** 26%  _(error: second discount missed)_

**Working**

1. 1.4 × 0.9 = 1.26
2. 1.26 × 0.95 = 1.197 ⇒ 19.7% profit

**Formula:** SP/CP = (1 + m) Π(1 − d)  
**Trap:** Successive discounts compound.

## QAA-095 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

By allowing a 12% discount a shopkeeper earns 10% profit. If he allows a 20% discount instead, he will:

- **A.** Lose 2%  _(error: sign flipped)_
- **B.** Gain 2%  _(error: discount change netted against profit)_
- **C.** Lose 8%  _(error: extra discount taken as loss)_
- **D.** Neither gain nor lose ✅

**Working**

1. MP/CP = 1.10/0.88 = 1.25
2. At 20% off: SP = 1.25 × 0.8 = 1.00 CP
3. No profit, no loss

**Formula:** MP/CP from the first condition  
**Trap:** Recompute the MP/CP ratio.

## QAA-096 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

An article sold at a 16% discount fetches ₹4,200 and gives a 5% profit. If it were sold at a 10% discount, the profit would be:

- **A.** 11%  _(error: 6-point discount cut added to profit)_
- **B.** 12.5% ✅
- **C.** 12.14%  _(error: extra ₹300 measured on old SP then added)_
- **D.** 10%  _(error: discount read as profit)_

**Working**

1. MP = 4,200/0.84 = 5,000; CP = 4,200/1.05 = 4,000
2. SP at 10% off = 4,500
3. Profit = 500/4,000 = 12.5%

**Formula:** Recover MP and CP separately  
**Trap:** Profit changes by more than the discount change.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-097 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

An article costs ₹1,600. At what price must it be marked so that after a 20% discount the seller still gains 25%?

- **A.** ₹2,000  _(error: discount ignored)_
- **B.** ₹2,320  _(error: markup = 25 + 20)_
- **C.** ₹2,500 ✅
- **D.** ₹2,400  _(error: 20% added instead of dividing by 0.8)_

**Working**

1. Required SP = 1,600 × 1.25 = 2,000
2. MP = 2,000/0.8 = 2,500

**Formula:** MP = CP(1 + p)/(1 − d)  
**Trap:** Grossing up for 20% off is 25%, not 20%.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-098 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

On a marked price of ₹12,000, scheme A offers successive discounts of 20% and 10%; scheme B offers a flat 25% discount. Which is better for the buyer, and by how much?

- **A.** Scheme A, by ₹600  _(error: A treated as flat 30%)_
- **B.** Scheme A, by ₹360 ✅
- **C.** Both cost the same  _(error: 28% and 25% confused)_
- **D.** Scheme B, by ₹360  _(error: comparison reversed)_

**Working**

1. A: 12,000 × 0.8 × 0.9 = 8,640
2. B: 12,000 × 0.75 = 9,000
3. A cheaper by 360

**Formula:** Equivalent of 20% and 10% = 28%  
**Trap:** Successive discounts are less than their sum but still beat 25% here.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-099 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

A shopkeeper gives one article free with every five bought and also allows a 10% discount on the marked price. He still gains 20%. The marked price is what percentage above cost?

- **A.** 63.64%  _(error: the two discounts added)_
- **B.** 60% ✅
- **C.** 66 2/3%  _(error: free article treated as a 20% discount)_
- **D.** 33 1/3%  _(error: free article ignored)_

**Working**

1. For 6 articles he receives 5 × 0.9 MP = 4.5 MP
2. Per article: 0.75 MP = 1.2 CP
3. MP = 1.6 CP ⇒ 60% above cost

**Formula:** Effective discount = 1 − (5/6)(0.9) = 25%  
**Trap:** 1 free with 5 is a 1/6 discount, not 1/5.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-100 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

The ratio of marked price to cost price of an item is 5 : 4. What is the maximum discount percentage the seller can allow and still earn a profit of at least 5%?

- **A.** 16% ✅
- **B.** 20%  _(error: break-even discount)_
- **C.** 25%  _(error: markup percentage reported)_
- **D.** 15%  _(error: 5% profit taken on MP)_

**Working**

1. CP 4, MP 5
2. Minimum SP = 4 × 1.05 = 4.2
3. Max discount = 0.8/5 = 16%

**Formula:** d = 1 − CP(1 + p)/MP  
**Trap:** Profit is on cost; discount is on MP.

## QAA-101 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

A shop used to sell at the marked price and earn 20% profit. It now raises the marked price by 30% and then offers a 30% discount. Its new profit percentage is:

- **A.** 9%  _(error: buyer's 9% saving read as profit)_
- **B.** 9.2% ✅
- **C.** 11%  _(error: 20 − 9 subtracted)_
- **D.** 20%  _(error: believes raise and discount cancel)_

**Working**

1. New SP = MP × 1.3 × 0.7 = 0.91 MP
2. MP = 1.2 CP ⇒ SP = 1.092 CP
3. Profit = 9.2%

**Formula:** SP/CP = 1.2 × 0.91  
**Trap:** Equal raise and discount leave a 9% net cut in price.

## QAA-102 · L3 · hard · Discount and marked price · officer

A store gives 25% off the marked price and a further ₹150 off any bill above ₹2,000 after that discount. A customer buys goods marked ₹3,000. The effective discount is:

- **A.** 28.75%  _(error: ₹150 deducted before the 25% discount)_
- **B.** 30% ✅
- **C.** 31.67%  _(error: ₹150 measured on discounted bill then added)_
- **D.** 25%  _(error: ₹150 off not applied)_

**Working**

1. After 25%: 2,250
2. After ₹150: 2,100
3. Discount = 900/3,000 = 30%

**Formula:** Effective discount = (MP − final bill)/MP

## QAA-103 · L1 · easy · Combined transactions and overall gain · foundation

A trader buys two items for ₹500 each. He sells one at 20% profit and the other at 10% loss. His overall result is:

- **C.** 10% profit  _(error: rates netted without averaging)_
- **D.** 5% profit ✅

**Working**

1. SP = 600 + 450 = 1,050 on cost 1,000
2. Profit = 5%

**Formula:** Equal costs ⇒ overall % = average of the two %  
**Trap:** Equal CPs give a simple average.

## QAA-104 · L2 · medium · Combined transactions and overall gain · foundation

A grocer mixes 20 kg of rice at ₹40/kg with 30 kg at ₹50/kg and sells the mixture at ₹54/kg. His profit percentage is:

- **A.** 20%  _(error: simple average cost ₹45 used)_
- **B.** 14.81%  _(error: profit measured on SP)_
- **C.** 8%  _(error: compared with ₹50 grade only)_
- **D.** 17.39% ✅

**Working**

1. Total cost = 800 + 1,500 = 2,300 for 50 kg ⇒ ₹46/kg
2. Profit = 8/46 = 17.39%

**Formula:** Weighted average cost  
**Trap:** Weights are 20 and 30 kg.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-105 · L2 · medium · Combined transactions and overall gain · foundation

A vendor buys 120 eggs at ₹6 each. 20 break, and he sells the rest at ₹8 each. His profit percentage is:

- **A.** 25%  _(error: ₹2 margin per ₹8 SP)_
- **B.** 11 1/9% ✅
- **C.** 10%  _(error: profit measured on SP)_
- **D.** 33 1/3%  _(error: breakage ignored)_

**Working**

1. Cost = 720; revenue = 100 × 8 = 800
2. Profit = 80/720 = 11 1/9%

**Formula:** Profit % = (Revenue − Total cost)/Total cost  
**Trap:** Broken eggs still cost money.

## QAA-106 · L2 · medium · Combined transactions and overall gain · foundation

- **B.** 17.81%  _(error: profit measured on total SP)_
- **C.** 21 2/3% ✅
- **D.** 20%  _(error: simple average of rates)_

**Working**

1. Profits: 30 + 80 + 150 = 260
2. 260/1,200 = 21 2/3%

**Formula:** Overall % = Σ profit ÷ Σ cost  
**Trap:** Weight by cost.

1. Cost = 20,000
2. Revenue = 15,000 + 7,200 = 22,200
3. Profit = 11%

## QAA-108 · L3 · hard · Combined transactions and overall gain · officer

Two horses cost ₹42,000 together. One is sold at a 20% loss and the other at a 30% gain, and overall there is neither gain nor loss. The cost of the horse sold at a loss is:

- **B.** ₹25,200 ✅
- **C.** ₹28,000  _(error: costs split 2 : 1)_
- **D.** ₹16,800  _(error: cost of the other horse)_

**Working**

1. 0.2x = 0.3(42,000 − x)
2. 0.5x = 12,600 ⇒ x = 25,200

**Formula:** Loss amount = gain amount

## QAA-109 · L3 · hard · Combined transactions and overall gain · officer

A trader sells 30 kg of an 80 kg stock at 12% profit. At what profit percentage must he sell the rest to earn 18% overall?

1. 30 × 12 + 50x = 80 × 18
2. 50x = 1,080 ⇒ x = 21.6%

## QAA-110 · L3 · hard · Combined transactions and overall gain · officer

A dealer buys 400 units at ₹25 each and pays ₹1,000 transport. 5% of the units are damaged and sold at half the cost price; the rest are sold at ₹32 each. His profit percentage is:

- **A.** 12.82% ✅
- **B.** 10.55%  _(error: damaged units written off entirely)_
- **C.** 24.1%  _(error: transport cost ignored)_
- **D.** 16.36%  _(error: damaged units sold at full price)_

**Working**

1. Total cost = 10,000 + 1,000 = 11,000
2. Revenue = 20 × 12.5 + 380 × 32 = 250 + 12,160 = 12,410
3. Profit = 1,410/11,000 = 12.82%

**Formula:** Include every cost and every sale

## QAA-111 · L3 · hard · Combined transactions and overall gain · officer

A merchant blends 30 kg of tea at ₹180/kg with 20 kg at ₹240/kg. To earn 25% on the blend, his selling price per kg should be:

- **A.** ₹255 ✅
- **B.** ₹262.50  _(error: unweighted average cost)_
- **C.** ₹272  _(error: 25% taken as margin on SP)_
- **D.** ₹225  _(error: cheaper tea's cost used)_

**Working**

1. Average cost = (5,400 + 4,800)/50 = ₹204
2. SP = 204 × 1.25 = ₹255

**Formula:** Weighted average cost × (1 + p)

## QAA-112 · L3 · hard · Combined transactions and overall gain · officer

A shopkeeper sells half his stock at 20% profit and one-third at 10% loss. At what profit percentage must he sell the rest to make 12% overall?

- **A.** 26%  _(error: unweighted average of three rates)_
- **B.** 5.33%  _(error: forgot to divide by the one-sixth share)_
- **C.** 32% ✅
- **D.** 62%  _(error: weights 1/2 and 1/3 swapped)_

**Working**

1. Rest = 1/6 of stock
2. (1/2)(20) − (1/3)(10) + (1/6)x = 12
3. 10 − 3.33 + x/6 = 12 ⇒ x = 32%

**Formula:** Σ share × rate = overall rate  
**Trap:** The last share is only 1/6.

2. Revenue = 54 × 36 = 1,944

## QAA-115 · L3 · hard · Combined transactions and overall gain · officer

1. 0.4 × 25 − 0.6 × 5 = 10 − 3 = 7
2. Overall profit 7%

## QAA-117 · L1 · easy · Equal profit and loss conditions · foundation

The profit on selling an article for ₹850 equals the loss on selling it for ₹650. Its cost price is:

- **A.** ₹800  _(error: rounded towards the higher SP)_
- **B.** ₹1,500  _(error: SPs added, not averaged)_
- **C.** ₹700  _(error: rounded towards the lower SP)_
- **D.** ₹750 ✅

**Working**

1. 850 − C = C − 650
2. C = 750

**Formula:** CP = average of the two SPs

## QAA-118 · L2 · medium · Equal profit and loss conditions · foundation

- **D.** 4% loss ✅

**Working**

1. CPs: 1,000 and 1,500; total 2,500
2. SP total 2,400 ⇒ loss 100 = 4%

**Formula:** Equal SP, ±x% ⇒ loss x²/100 %

## QAA-119 · L1 · easy · Equal profit and loss conditions · foundation

The loss on selling a table for ₹1,340 equals the profit on selling it for ₹1,780. At what price should it be sold to gain 25%?

- **A.** ₹1,560  _(error: cost price reported)_
- **B.** ₹2,225  _(error: 25% applied to the higher SP)_
- **C.** ₹1,950 ✅
- **D.** ₹1,675  _(error: 25% applied to the lower SP)_

**Working**

1. CP = (1,340 + 1,780)/2 = 1,560
2. SP = 1,560 × 1.25 = 1,950

**Formula:** CP = average of SPs  
**Trap:** Find CP first.

## QAA-120 · L2 · medium · Equal profit and loss conditions · foundation

The profit on selling an item for ₹1,440 is twice the loss on selling it for ₹960. The cost price is:

- **A.** ₹1,120 ✅
- **B.** ₹1,600  _(error: SPs added then scaled)_
- **C.** ₹1,200  _(error: profit taken equal to loss)_
- **D.** ₹1,280  _(error: factor 2 put on the profit side)_

**Working**

1. 1,440 − C = 2(C − 960)
2. 3C = 3,360 ⇒ C = 1,120

**Formula:** Profit = k × Loss

## QAA-121 · L2 · medium · Equal profit and loss conditions · foundation

Selling a lamp for ₹510 causes a 15% loss. To gain 15%, it should be sold for:

- **A.** ₹663  _(error: 30% added to ₹510)_
- **B.** ₹586.50  _(error: 15% added to the loss-making SP)_
- **C.** ₹690 ✅
- **D.** ₹600  _(error: cost price reported)_

**Working**

1. CP = 510/0.85 = 600
2. SP = 600 × 1.15 = 690

**Formula:** CP = SP ÷ (1 − loss%)

## QAA-122 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

Two lots of goods are sold for ₹4,800 each, one at 25% profit and the other at 25% loss. The overall loss in rupees is:

- **A.** ₹640 ✅
- **B.** ₹600  _(error: 6.25% applied to total SP)_
- **C.** ₹1,200  _(error: 25% of one SP)_
- **D.** ₹0  _(error: equal rates assumed to cancel)_

**Working**

1. CPs: 4,800/1.25 = 3,840 and 4,800/0.75 = 6,400
2. Total CP 10,240 vs SP 9,600
3. Loss = ₹640

## QAA-123 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

Two articles have the same cost. One is sold at 12% profit and the other at 8% loss; the difference in their selling prices is ₹1,000. The cost of each is:

- **A.** ₹25,000  _(error: difference taken as (12 − 8)%)_

- **C.** ₹8,333.33  _(error: difference taken as 12%)_
- **D.** ₹5,000 ✅

**Working**

1. SP difference = (1.12 − 0.92)C = 0.20C
2. 0.2C = 1,000 ⇒ C = 5,000

**Formula:** SP gap = (p + l)% of CP  
**Trap:** Profit and loss percentages add across the gap.

## QAA-124 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

- **A.** Loss of ₹2 lakh ✅

- **C.** Loss of ₹1.98 lakh  _(error: 1% applied to total SP)_
- **D.** No gain, no loss  _(error: equal rates assumed to cancel)_

**Working**

1. CPs: 99/1.1 = 90 and 99/0.9 = 110 (₹ lakh)
2. Total CP 200 vs SP 198
3. Loss ₹2 lakh

## QAA-125 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

- **C.** ₹1,500 ✅
- **D.** ₹1,800  _(error: price at 20% profit)_

**Working**

1. (1.2 − 0.9)C = 450
2. C = 1,500 = no-loss price

## QAA-126 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

A trader sells two items at a gain of p% on one and a loss of p% on the other (p > 0). Consider:

I. If the two items cost the same, he neither gains nor loses overall.
II. If the two items sell for the same price, he always makes an overall loss.
III. With equal selling prices, the overall loss equals p²/100 per cent of the total selling price.

Which are correct?

- **A.** I and II only ✅
- **B.** II and III only  _(error: I wrongly rejected)_
- **C.** I, II and III  _(error: III: loss is p²/100 % of total cost, not of total SP)_
- **D.** I only  _(error: II wrongly rejected)_

**Working**

1. I: gains and losses are equal amounts on equal costs — true
2. II: equal SPs force a larger cost on the loss item — true
3. III: p = 20, SP 120 each ⇒ CPs 100, 150; loss 10 = 4% of cost 250 but 4.17% of SP 240 — false

**Formula:** Equal SP ⇒ loss% = p²/100 on cost  
**Trap:** Check the base of the p²/100 rule.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:


---

## QAA-127 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

Two watches are sold for ₹2,000 each, one at 20% profit. If the trader neither gains nor loses overall, the loss percentage on the other watch is:

- **A.** 20%  _(error: equal rates assumed)_
- **B.** 16 2/3%  _(error: loss measured on SP)_
- **C.** 14 2/7% ✅
- **D.** 10%  _(error: half of 20% taken)_

**Working**

1. CP₁ = 2,000/1.2 = 1,666 2/3
2. Total CP = 4,000 ⇒ CP₂ = 2,333 1/3
3. Loss = 333 1/3 / 2,333 1/3 = 14 2/7%

**Formula:** Loss amount = profit amount  
**Trap:** The loss item costs more, so its loss % is smaller.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-128 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

- **A.** ₹828 ✅

**Working**

1. (1.05 − 0.80)C = 180 ⇒ C = 720

## QAA-129 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

Two items are each sold for ₹600. On one the profit equals 20% of its selling price; on the other the loss equals 20% of its selling price. The overall result is:

- **A.** Loss of ₹48  _(error: 4% of 1,200 applied)_
- **B.** Neither profit nor loss ✅
- **C.** Loss of 4%  _(error: p²/100 rule applied to a margin-on-SP case)_
- **D.** Gain of 4%  _(error: sign reversed)_

**Working**

1. Profit item: CP = 600 − 120 = 480
2. Loss item: CP = 600 + 120 = 720
3. Total CP 1,200 = total SP 1,200

## QAA-130 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

## QAA-131 · L3 · hard · Equal profit and loss conditions · officer

The profit when an item is sold for ₹1,920 is 60% more than the loss when it is sold for ₹1,400. The cost price is:

- **A.** ₹1,660  _(error: profit taken equal to loss)_
- **B.** ₹1,600 ✅

- **D.** ₹1,771.43  _(error: '60% more' read as 40% of)_

1. 1,920 − C = 1.6(C − 1,400)

## QAA-132 · L1 · easy · Simple and compound interest · foundation

## QAA-133 · L1 · easy · Simple and compound interest · foundation

The compound interest on ₹10,000 at 10% per annum for 2 years, compounded annually, is:

- **A.** ₹1,000  _(error: one year only)_
- **B.** ₹12,100  _(error: amount reported)_
- **C.** ₹2,100 ✅
- **D.** ₹2,000  _(error: simple interest)_

**Working**

1. A = 10,000 × 1.1² = 12,100
2. CI = 2,100

**Formula:** CI = P[(1 + r)^n − 1]

## QAA-135 · L2 · medium · Simple and compound interest · foundation

The difference between compound and simple interest on a sum for 2 years at 5% per annum is ₹40. The sum is:

- **D.** ₹800  _(error: 40 divided by 5%)_

**Working**

1. Difference = P(r/100)² = P × 0.0025
2. P = 40/0.0025 = 16,000

**Formula:** CI − SI (2 yrs) = P(r/100)²

## QAA-136 · L2 · medium · Simple and compound interest · foundation

The amount on ₹5,000 at 20% per annum for 2 years, compounded half-yearly, is:

1. Half-yearly rate 10%, periods 4
2. 5,000 × 1.1⁴ = 7,320.50

**Formula:** A = P(1 + r/2)^(2n)

## QAA-137 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

A sum at compound interest amounts to ₹8,820 in 2 years and ₹9,261 in 3 years. The sum is:

- **C.** ₹8,379  _(error: third-year interest subtracted once)_
- **D.** ₹8,400  _(error: only one year undone)_

**Working**

1. Rate = 441/8,820 = 5%
2. P = 8,820/1.05² = 8,000

**Formula:** r = (A₃ − A₂)/A₂

## QAA-138 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

The difference between compound and simple interest on ₹15,000 for 3 years at 10% per annum is:

- **A.** ₹150  _(error: 2-year difference formula used)_
- **B.** ₹465 ✅
- **C.** ₹4,965  _(error: CI reported)_
- **D.** ₹450  _(error: 2-year difference tripled)_

**Working**

1. SI = 4,500
2. CI = 15,000 × 0.331 = 4,965
3. Difference = 465

**Formula:** CI − SI (3 yrs) = P r²(3 + r)  
**Trap:** The 3-year gap is not three times the 2-year gap.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-139 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

A sum at simple interest amounts to ₹7,280 in 3 years and ₹8,400 in 5 years. The rate of interest is:

- **A.** 10% ✅
- **B.** 6.67%  _(error: yearly interest divided by 5-year amount)_
- **C.** 7.69%  _(error: yearly interest divided by 3-year amount)_
- **D.** 8%  _(error: ₹560 divided by a rounded principal of ₹7,000)_

**Working**

1. Interest for 2 years = 1,120 ⇒ 560 a year
2. P = 7,280 − 1,680 = 5,600
3. R = 560/5,600 = 10%

**Formula:** Yearly SI = ΔA ÷ Δt  
**Trap:** Rate is on the principal.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-140 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

₹20,000 is lent partly at 8% and partly at 12% simple interest. The total yearly interest is ₹1,960. The amount lent at 8% is:

- **A.** ₹10,000  _(error: equal split assumed)_
- **B.** ₹12,000  _(error: interest taken as ₹1,920)_
- **C.** ₹9,000  _(error: rates swapped)_
- **D.** ₹11,000 ✅

**Working**

1. 0.08x + 0.12(20,000 − x) = 1,960
2. 2,400 − 0.04x = 1,960 ⇒ x = 11,000

**Formula:** Alligation on rates: average 9.8%  
**Trap:** Larger share goes to the rate nearer 9.8%.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-141 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

At compound interest a sum becomes 3 times itself in 5 years. In how many years will it become 27 times itself?

- **A.** 65 years  _(error: treated as simple interest)_
- **B.** 15 years ✅
- **C.** 45 years  _(error: 27/3 × 5)_
- **D.** 10 years  _(error: only two tripling periods)_

**Working**

1. 27 = 3³
2. Three tripling periods = 15 years

**Formula:** (1 + r)^(kn) = 3^k  
**Trap:** Compounding multiplies; simple interest adds.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-142 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

At what rate of compound interest per annum will ₹6,250 amount to ₹7,290 in 2 years?

- **A.** 8% ✅
- **B.** 16.64%  _(error: total growth over 2 years)_
- **C.** 9%  _(error: rough square root)_
- **D.** 8.32%  _(error: simple-interest rate)_

**Working**

1. 7,290/6,250 = 1.1664 = 1.08²
2. Rate = 8%

**Formula:** (1 + r)² = A/P  
**Trap:** Take the square root of the growth factor.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-143 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

A loan of ₹50,000 at 10% compound interest per annum is repaid in two equal annual instalments. Each instalment is:

- **A.** ₹28,695.65  _(error: second instalment discounted at simple interest)_
- **B.** ₹27,500  _(error: SI for one year then halved)_
- **C.** ₹28,809.52 ✅
- **D.** ₹30,250  _(error: 2-year amount split equally)_

**Working**

1. x/1.1 + x/1.21 = 50,000
2. x = 50,000 × 1.21/2.1 = 28,809.52

**Formula:** Loan = Σ PV of instalments  
**Trap:** Discount each instalment for its own period.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-144 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

The simple interest on a sum for 3 years at 12% per annum is ₹5,400. The compound interest on the same sum for 2 years at 10% per annum is:

- **A.** ₹3,000  _(error: SI at 10% for 2 years)_
- **B.** ₹3,150 ✅
- **C.** ₹3,600  _(error: SI at 12% for 2 years)_
- **D.** ₹3,816  _(error: CI at 12% instead of 10%)_

**Working**

1. P = 5,400 × 100/36 = 15,000
2. CI = 15,000 × 0.21 = 3,150

**Formula:** SI → P; then CI = P[(1 + r)² − 1]  
**Trap:** Switch rates after finding P.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-145 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

A sum earns simple interest at 6% per annum for the first 2 years and 9% per annum for the next 3 years. The total interest is ₹3,900. The sum is:

- **A.** ₹10,000 ✅
- **B.** ₹10,400  _(error: average rate 7.5% × 5 years)_
- **C.** ₹26,000  _(error: rates added (15%) and used once)_
- **D.** ₹13,000  _(error: 6% and 9% each applied for 2 years only (30%))_

**Working**

1. Total rate = 6 × 2 + 9 × 3 = 39%
2. P = 3,900/0.39 = 10,000

**Formula:** SI = P Σ(rᵢtᵢ)  
**Trap:** Weight each rate by its years.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-146 · L3 · hard · Simple and compound interest · officer

On ₹40,000 at 20% per annum for 1 year, the compound interest compounded half-yearly exceeds that compounded annually by:

- **A.** ₹800  _(error: (r/2)² taken as 2%)_
- **B.** ₹200  _(error: used 5% per half-year)_
- **C.** ₹0  _(error: believes same annual rate gives same interest)_
- **D.** ₹400 ✅

**Working**

1. Half-yearly: 40,000 × 1.1² = 48,400 ⇒ CI 8,400
2. Annual: 8,000
3. Difference = 400

**Formula:** Extra = P(r/2)²  
**Trap:** Interest on the first half-year's interest.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-147 · L1 · easy · Ages — present, past and future · foundation

The present ages of two cousins are in the ratio 4 : 5. After 5 years the ratio will be 5 : 6. The elder cousin's present age is:

- **A.** 25 years ✅
- **B.** 35 years  _(error: future ratio term used as present multiple)_
- **C.** 30 years  _(error: age after 5 years)_
- **D.** 20 years  _(error: younger cousin's age)_

**Working**

1. (4x + 5)/(5x + 5) = 5/6
2. 24x + 30 = 25x + 25 ⇒ x = 5
3. Elder = 25 years

**Formula:** Add the same years to both ages  
**Trap:** Years are added to ages, not to ratio terms.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-148 · L1 · easy · Ages — present, past and future · foundation

A father is three times as old as his son. After 10 years he will be twice as old as the son. The son's present age is:

- **A.** 30 years  _(error: father's age reported)_
- **B.** 20 years  _(error: son's age after 10 years)_
- **C.** 10 years ✅
- **D.** 15 years  _(error: 10 added to one side only)_

**Working**

1. 3s + 10 = 2(s + 10)
2. s = 10

**Formula:** Future: (F + t) = k(S + t)  
**Trap:** Both ages grow by 10.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-149 · L2 · medium · Ages — present, past and future · foundation

The sum of the present ages of A and B is 58 years. Five years ago their ages were in the ratio 3 : 5. B's present age is:

- **A.** 36.25 years  _(error: ratio applied to present sum)_
- **B.** 23 years  _(error: A's age reported)_
- **C.** 30 years  _(error: age five years ago)_
- **D.** 35 years ✅

**Working**

1. Sum five years ago = 58 − 10 = 48
2. B then = 48 × 5/8 = 30
3. B now = 35

**Formula:** Reduce the sum by 5 for each person  
**Trap:** Ratio holds five years ago, not now.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-150 · L2 · medium · Ages — present, past and future · foundation

A is 6 years older than B. Four years ago A was twice as old as B. B's present age is:

- **A.** 16 years  _(error: A's age reported)_
- **B.** 8 years  _(error: 4 subtracted from one side only)_
- **C.** 10 years ✅
- **D.** 6 years  _(error: B's age four years ago)_

**Working**

1. A − 4 = 2(B − 4), A = B + 6
2. B + 2 = 2B − 8 ⇒ B = 10

**Formula:** Age difference is constant  
**Trap:** Subtract 4 from both ages.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-151 · L2 · medium · Ages — present, past and future · foundation

Ten years ago, P was half as old as Q. Their present ages are in the ratio 3 : 4. The sum of their present ages is:

- **A.** 45 years  _(error: 10 added only once)_
- **B.** 70 years  _(error: multiplier taken as 10)_
- **C.** 35 years ✅
- **D.** 15 years  _(error: sum ten years ago)_

**Working**

1. 2(3x − 10) = 4x − 10 ⇒ x = 5
2. Ages 15 and 20; sum 35

**Formula:** Past ages = present − 10  
**Trap:** Both ages lose 10.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-152 · L3 · hard · Ages — present, past and future · officer

The present ages of P and Q are in the ratio 7 : 9. Eight years ago the ratio was 5 : 7. What will be the ratio of their ages 12 years from now?

- **A.** 19:21  _(error: 12 added to ratio terms)_
- **B.** 7:9  _(error: present ratio assumed unchanged)_
- **C.** 5:6 ✅
- **D.** 10:9  _(error: 12 added to P's age only)_

**Working**

1. (7x − 8)/(9x − 8) = 5/7 ⇒ 4x = 16 ⇒ x = 4
2. Ages 28 and 36
3. In 12 years: 40 : 48 = 5 : 6

**Formula:** Find actual ages first  
**Trap:** Adding years to ratio terms is wrong.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-153 · L3 · hard · Ages — present, past and future · officer

At their wedding, the average age of a husband and wife was 25 years. Six years later, with one child, the family's average age is 22 years. The child's age is:

- **A.** 6 years  _(error: years since marriage)_
- **B.** 16 years  _(error: couple's ageing ignored)_
- **C.** 10 years  _(error: only one spouse aged 6 years)_
- **D.** 4 years ✅

**Working**

1. Couple's sum at wedding = 50; after 6 years = 62
2. Family sum = 3 × 22 = 66
3. Child = 66 − 62 = 4

**Formula:** Sum = average × count; each person ages  
**Trap:** Both spouses age by 6.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-154 · L3 · hard · Ages — present, past and future · officer

A mother and daughter have a combined age of 50 years. In 5 years the mother's age will be 7/3 of the daughter's. When was the mother four times as old as the daughter?

- **A.** 5 years ago ✅
- **B.** 8 years ago  _(error: daughter's age then read as time)_
- **C.** 5 years hence  _(error: direction of time reversed)_
- **D.** 3.75 years ago  _(error: t not subtracted from mother's age)_

**Working**

1. M + D = 50; 3(M + 5) = 7(D + 5) ⇒ D = 13, M = 37
2. 37 − t = 4(13 − t) ⇒ 3t = 15 ⇒ t = 5

**Formula:** Subtract t from both ages  
**Trap:** Solve present ages first.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:

## QAA-155 · L3 · hard · Ages — present, past and future · officer

The ages of A and B are in the ratio 3 : 4, and those of B and C in the ratio 6 : 7. If the three ages total 105 years, C's age is:

- **A.** 52.5 years  _(error: chain not equalised (3 : 4 : 7))_
- **B.** 45.94 years  _(error: chain written as 3 : 6 : 7)_
- **C.** 36 years  _(error: B's age reported)_
- **D.** 42 years ✅

**Working**

1. A : B : C = 9 : 12 : 14
2. C = 105 × 14/35 = 42

**Formula:** Equalise the common term B  
**Trap:** Make B's terms equal (12) before combining.

**Reviewer:** ☐ key ☐ stem ☐ distractors ☐ level ☐ fact — notes:
